Cutting 64 of 256 Experts from a GGUF by Byte Copy: What the Headers Say About GLM-5.3-SLIM on One B200

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 11 • Originally published at ginigen-ai-edge-ai-news.static.hf.space

Scantist's GLM-5.3-SLIM-GGUF is a 192-expert version of Unsloth's 256-expert UD-IQ2_M file, and it is built by copying bytes, not by requantizing. We read both sets of GGUF headers on November 11, 2026. The file drops from 238.58 GB to 179.86 GB, a 24.6% cut, and the header math says 93.4% of that cut is the expert slice, 6.4% is a removed next-token-prediction (MTP) layer and 0.2% is router rows. Every one of the 225 routed-expert tensors in the new file is exactly 0.75 times the size of its source tensor, in the same quant type, and all 1,407 other non-router tensors keep their names, shapes and types.

The practical point for anyone sizing hardware: the cut buys fit, not speed. Both files read about 21.5 GB of weights per decoded token, because top-8 routing is unchanged. On one B200 the smaller file leaves roughly 11.6 GiB for everything else, which is the difference between "does not load" and "about 100K tokens of shared KV cache".

The repository Scantist/GLM-5.3-SLIM-GGUF was created on November 2, 2026. When we read it on November 11 it showed 1,303 likes and 11,066 downloads. It holds six shards of one model plus an importance-matrix file and three evaluation scripts. The base is unsloth/GLM-5.3-GGUF, folder UD-IQ2_M, which also has six shards.

We parsed the tensor headers of all twelve shards with HTTP range requests, so no weights were downloaded. Here is what the headers say.

The average bits per weight goes up slightly even though nothing was requantized. That is not a contradiction. The experts are stored at about 2.42 bits and most other tensors at 4 to 8 bits, so when you remove a quarter of the experts, the high-precision tensors weigh a little more in the average.

Cutting 64 of 256 experts sounds like a clean 25%. The headers show why it lands a bit lower on the file and higher on the experts. We split the 58,716,322,560 bytes of tensor savings into three pieces.

The three rows add up to the byte. The expert slice is exactly 25% of the routed-expert bytes in those 75 layers (54.84 of 219.37 GB), but those experts are only 92% of the original file, because attention, shared experts, embeddings and the output matrix do not shrink. A quarter of 92% is 23.0 points. The MTP layer then adds 1.57 points by disappearing altogether, which is how the total reaches 24.6%.

In GGUF the expert axis is the last, slowest-varying dimension of each routed-expert tensor. The header for blk.20.ffn_down_exps.weight lists dimensions [2048, 6144, 256] in the source and [2048, 6144, 192] in the new file. One expert is therefore one contiguous byte range of the same length in both files, and keeping experts means copying ranges.

For the IQ3_XXS type used by that tensor, a block of 256 weights takes 98 bytes, so one expert is 2048 x 6144 / 256 x 98 = 4,816,896 bytes. Two more checks make the claim hard to argue with:

That matches what the model card says: kept experts are copied byte for byte, in their original order, as experts 0 to 191. The card also reports that router rows are bit-identical to those of an independent NVFP4 checkpoint built with the same expert selection. We did not verify that part, since it needs the weights.

The removed MTP layer has a quirk worth knowing. Block 78 in the Unsloth file stores its experts as Q3_K (down) and Q2_K (gate and up), while every other layer uses IQ types. It is the only place those two types appear, so the "0.75 times" rule holds for the 75 kept layers and the MTP layer has to be accounted for separately.

No, and the header math shows why. A token only reads the experts the router picks. With 8 of 256 or 8 of 192, the bytes per expert are the same, so the weight bytes read per token barely move. Counting every tensor except the token embedding (a row lookup) and taking 8 experts per MoE layer:

Scantist reports 34.5 tokens per second for a single sequence on one B200 with an f16 KV cache. Multiplied by 21.5 GB per token, that is an implied 743 GB/s of weight traffic. NVIDIA lists B200 memory bandwidth at about 8 TB/s, so the single-sequence run uses roughly 9% of it. The model card says as much: the speeds are limited by the llama.cpp kernels for these low-bit types, not by memory bandwidth. Our figure ignores KV reads and activations, so treat it as a rough ceiling check, not a profile.

The consequence for edge and on-prem teams is simple. Dropping experts is a way to make a model fit, and then batching is what buys throughput:

Throughput climbs from 34.5 tok/s with one sequence to 203.3 tok/s with 64, most likely because the weights read in one step are shared across all sequences in the batch. These numbers come from Scantist's llama-batched-bench runs, not ours.

The model card reports 183,359 MiB on one B200 and 170,896 MiB of weights on the GPU (the 624 MiB token embedding stays in host memory). Our header total for the E192 tensors is 171,520 MiB, which agrees once you subtract that embedding. The 256-expert file is 227,516 MiB, which overshoots the card's memory figure by 44,157 MiB.

KV cache per token is where we can check the card against the config. The metadata gives a latent width of 512 plus 64 rotary dimensions, so one cached vector is 576 values, or 1,152 bytes at f16. Across 78 layers that is 89,856 bytes, or 87.75 KiB per token, which matches the card's MLA figure exactly. The card adds 5.25 KiB per token for the DSA indexer for a total of 93 KiB. We could not derive the indexer share from the headers, so that part is the card's number. At q4_0 the same arithmetic gives 24.68 KiB for MLA, and adding the card's indexer figure scaled by the 18/64 q4_0 ratio gives 26.16 KiB, close to the 26.2 KiB the card lists.

By our arithmetic the f16 pool costs 9,300 MiB at 102,400 tokens, so about 970 MiB of the reported usage is something other than weights, the 512 MiB compute buffer and KV. At 110,592 tokens that remainder is about 1,000 MiB. The q4_0 run carries about 2,041 MiB, so do not extrapolate the overhead linearly.

One long reasoning request of 68,608 tokens is 6.08 GiB at f16, which is 67% of the 102,400-token pool. That is why the card suggests q4_0 for long-reasoning workloads: the same request costs 1.71 GiB and five fit. The price, per the card, is 11% lower single-sequence speed at 32K tokens of context and 20% lower at 63K.

Scantist reports these results from its own scripts:

The card is candid about the limit of this evidence: the 256-expert UD-IQ2_M and the full-precision model were not run on the same harness, so none of these numbers measures what dropping 64 experts costs. The serial and concurrent HumanEval runs differ by 9 discordant problems (exact McNemar p = 0.18), which is a useful reminder of how much batch numerics alone can move a score. The card also warns that the earlier FP8 tests of the same expert set showed a clear loss on Chinese knowledge QA and a smaller loss on science QA.

The cosine similarities in the card's integrity section (0.94 for IQ2_XXS, 0.98 for IQ3_XXS, 0.994 for IQ4_XS) show that the kept experts map to the right source experts, against under 0.003 for a neighbouring expert. They are not a quality score for the pruned model.

The script below reads one shard header from each repository with a 4 MB range request and prints the shape and byte count of the same expert tensor. It needs only the Python standard library.

Output from our run on November 11, 2026:

192 / 256 = 0.75, and 924,844,032 / 1,233,125,376 = 0.75 exactly. To reproduce the full tables, loop the same parser over all six shards of each repository, sum bytes by tensor name, and compare each expert tensor against its source.

We did not run the model, so every speed, memory-use and benchmark figure above is Scantist's. Our own numbers are the file sizes, tensor bytes, parameter counts, per-token weight bytes and KV arithmetic, all derived from the Hugging Face API and the GGUF headers. We did not reproduce the claim that a 192 GB Mac can run the file. The weights leave 24.5 GiB of headroom on a 192 GiB machine, but macOS caps how much unified memory the GPU may use by default, so check that limit before you plan around it. The per-token read figure also leaves out KV reads and activations.

If you are choosing between a smaller expert count and a smaller bit width, do the header math first. A byte-slice like this one is cheap, reversible and lossless with respect to the source file, but it is a lossy change to the model, and the evidence for its cost is thin here. Separate three questions that are easy to blur: does the file fit, how much context is left, and how fast does a token come out. Pruning experts moves the first two. Batching and kernels move the third.

Does removing experts make GLM-5.3 faster? Not by itself. Top-8 routing reads the same number of expert bytes per token, about 6.86 GB in both files, and the whole weight read is about 21.5 GB per token. The gain is that the model fits on one 192 GB-class GPU.

Why is the saving 24.6% and not 25%? The expert slice removes exactly 25% of the routed-expert bytes in 75 layers, which is 23.0 points of the file because attention, embeddings and shared tensors stay. The removed MTP layer adds 1.57 points. The three pieces sum to 58,716,322,560 bytes.

Is the 192-expert file requantized? The header evidence says no. All 225 routed-expert tensors keep their quant type and are exactly 0.75 times the size of their source, and the other tensors keep their names, shapes and types.

How much KV cache does one token cost? The MLA part is 87.75 KiB at f16 by our arithmetic from the config (576 values, 2 bytes, 78 layers). The card lists 93 KiB with the DSA indexer, and 26.2 KiB at q4_0.

Does the pruned model score well? Scantist reports 84.8% on GPQA-Diamond and 92.7% on HumanEval, but did not run the 256-expert baseline on the same harness, so the cost of the pruning is not measured.

Which numbers here are our own? File sizes, tensor bytes, parameters, per-token weight bytes and KV arithmetic. Speeds, memory use and benchmark scores are Scantist's.

Sources: Hugging Face repositories Scantist/GLM-5.3-SLIM-GGUF and unsloth/GLM-5.3-GGUF, and the base model zai-org/GLM-5.3. File sizes, tensor types and metadata were read on November 11, 2026.

The Allowlist Guarded the Destination, Not the Route: A Proxies Parameter Leaked Credentials From an Agent HTTP Tool

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agent HTTP tools have a problem that ordinary HTTP clients do not: the caller is a language model that reads untrusted text. When an agent fetches a web page, a ticket or a README, whatever is in that content can try to steer the next tool call. That is indirect prompt injection, and it is the threat the advisory describes.

The Strands http_request tool let operators attach secrets to requests safely, in theory. You listed an environment variable and the hostnames it may be sent to, like this (the example comes from the tool's own docstring):

The model then passed auth_env_var="GITHUB_TOKEN" and a URL. The tool refused any URL whose hostname was not in the list. That control is sound for what it covers: the model cannot ask for the GitHub token to be sent to evil.example.

The gap was a second input the model could also set. The PR description puts it plainly: the proxies parameter "is included in the LLM-controllable input schema, allowing an LLM influenced by indirect prompt injection to route credentialed requests through actor-controlled proxy infrastructure, bypassing the HTTP_REQUEST_TOKEN_CONFIG hostname allowlist." The AWS bulletin adds the consequence: "The credential is attached, and the request is routed through the actor's proxy on the first hop, disclosing the credential in cleartext."

The GitHub advisory rates it Moderate with CVSS 6.9 and classifies it as CWE-863, incorrect authorization. At least one vulnerability aggregator lists 7.4. Scorers disagree, so read the vector and your own exposure, not the number. The advisory also notes a second weakness in the same neighborhood: GET requests bypassed the consent gate. I confirmed that in the v0.8.1 source, where the confirmation dialog applies only to POST, PUT, PATCH and DELETE. A credentialed GET to a hostile route therefore needed no human in the loop.

Because it guarded the destination, and a credential's journey has more than one position. Before the request reaches api.github.com, it passes through whatever the client was told to use as a proxy. That first hop sees the request, and the allowlist had no say over it.

This is the same shape as the lesson from the symlink and trailing-dot bugs I covered earlier: a check is only as strong as the set of inputs it actually constrains. Here the check constrained the URL's hostname, while the egress route, the scheme and the port were all outside it.

Here is the check in v0.8.1, trimmed to the part that matters:

.hostname drops the scheme and the port. So http://api.github.com/user and https://api.github.com:8443/user both pass a list containing api.github.com. Whether that matters depends on what the credential rides on, so I measured it rather than guessing.

I wrote a short script that starts throwaway listeners on 127.0.0.1 and uses the requests library (2.34.2) with a fake token. Nothing leaves the machine. The relevant part:

The listener for case 1 received this, secret included:

The listener for case 2 received only CONNECT api.github.com:443 HTTP/1.1 and Host: api.github.com:443. The secret was not in it, because with certificate verification on, the headers travel inside the TLS tunnel.

That second result refines the headline, it does not contradict it. The bulletin's "cleartext" statement holds when the approved hostname is reached over plain HTTP, and a hostname-only check does not stop that. The tool also gates disabling certificate verification behind a separate environment variable (STRANDS_HTTP_ALLOW_INSECURE_SSL), which is why the TLS case stayed closed in my run. I did not test every deployment shape, and I did not run the attack against a real Strands agent. Treat the experiment as a demonstration of the mechanism, and the advisory as the authority on the product.

I also checked one more egress knob that remained after the patch: redirects. The tool exposes allow_redirects, and requests follows them by default. Against a second local listener on a different port, the Authorization header was stripped on the cross-origin hop, but a custom X-API-Key header was forwarded. The tool supports auth_type="api_key", which sets exactly that header. This is general requests behavior, not a reported Strands flaw, and exploiting it would need an open redirect on an approved host. I include it because it shows how many places "where does this credential go" can be decided.

Small, which is the instructive part. PR #513 changed two files, with 14 additions and 26 deletions. It deleted the proxies property from the tool's JSON schema, deleted the doc example that showed a proxy, and stopped forwarding tool_input.get("proxies") to requests. The test that asserted "the proxy was passed through" now asserts the opposite: an LLM-supplied proxies field must not reach requests.

I extracted the TOOL_SPEC from both tagged versions and diffed the property names:

The last line is my own classification of which remaining properties influence where a request goes or how it is protected. I am not claiming any of them is exploitable. The point is that after a fix like this, the right question is the one the patch author implicitly asked: which knobs on this tool should a model ever hold? The patch's own comment answers it for proxies: "Proxies are an operator/deployment concern, not something the model should choose."

Here is a runnable model of three gate designs against five credential-leaking requests and two legitimate ones. It is illustrative, not a benchmark of any product, and gate C is my design, not what Strands shipped (they removed the knob).

Three observations. Gate A, the hostname check, stops only the case everybody thinks of. Gate B closes three more by comparing the whole origin, and it costs nothing on legitimate traffic. Gate C closes the last one, the route, and it does so by removing the model's say over routing rather than by inspecting the route.

That last design principle is the one to carry away. You can try to validate a model-supplied proxy URL, but the validation has to know every host you consider safe, every scheme, every redirect, and every environment variable the HTTP library reads. Taking the parameter out of the schema is a smaller attack surface than any validator, and it is the option the maintainers chose.

A cheap regression guard is a CI test that fails when someone adds a property to a credentialed tool:

When the test fails, a person has to decide whether the model should hold the new knob. That one review step would have caught proxies the day it was added.

Because the vulnerable tool's home is closing. From the release notes:

Across v0.8.9 to v0.8.12 (September 15 to November 2), the release notes list 34 tools by name as deprecated, including python_repl, mcp_client, browser, code_interpreter, use_computer, file_write, editor and use_aws. The notes do not give a reason for the deprecations, and I will not guess one. The v0.9.0 notes say the same capabilities now ship as built-in tools in the Strands Harness SDK, with a migration guide linked from the release.

Practical consequences for anyone running it:

What is CVE-2026-18394? An incorrect-authorization flaw (CWE-863) in the http_request tool of strands-agents-tools before 0.8.2. The model could set a proxies parameter, so an indirect prompt injection could route a credentialed request through an attacker-controlled proxy and bypass the hostname allowlist for HTTP_REQUEST_TOKEN_CONFIG.

Which versions are affected and what is the fix? Versions before 0.8.2 are affected. Version 0.8.2 removes proxies from the model-visible input. Proxies are now configured only through HTTP_PROXY and HTTPS_PROXY in the environment, or in code. The advisory also recommends rotating credentials configured on affected versions.

Would HTTPS have protected the token? In my local test, a proxy on an https:// request saw only the CONNECT line and not the Authorization header, because certificate verification was on. The bulletin's cleartext scenario applies when the approved hostname is requested over plain HTTP, which a hostname-only allowlist permits. Do not rely on TLS to rescue a gate that ignores the scheme.

Is an allowlist enough for credentialed tools? Only if it covers the origin and the route. A hostname list alone does not constrain scheme, port, proxy or redirects. Pin the exact origin, keep routing out of the model's hands, and add consent for credentialed requests regardless of HTTP method.

Should I upgrade to v0.9.0? Not as a security fix. The release deprecates the package and turns every tool into an import error. Stay on a patched version only as a bridge, and plan the migration to the replacement tools, reviewing their parameters before you give them credentials.

Anthropic's IPO filing, line by line: how the $900.9B compute figure breaks down, and what $84.6B covers

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic launched its IPO roadshow on November 10: 825,000,000 Class A shares at an expected $70.00 to $78.00, listing on Nasdaq as ANT. The press has mostly repeated two big numbers, a raise of "more than $61 billion" and compute commitments of "about $900 billion." I read the filing itself (Amendment No. 2 to the S-1, filed with the SEC the same day) and the numbers decompose in ways the headlines do not show.

Everything marked "my arithmetic" is a calculation from filed numbers at the $74.00 midpoint. This is analysis of a preliminary prospectus, not investment advice, and the price range can still change.

The terms below come from Anthropic's roadshow announcement and the S-1/A No. 2. They match each other.

Two things here are easy to miss. First, the buyers in the private placement are mostly suppliers: chip makers, a memory maker, a foundry and cloud providers. That is a circular structure worth noting, because some of the money raised will flow back to the same companies as compute payments. The filing does not say that in so many words, so treat it as my reading rather than a stated fact. Second, the placement shares are Class N stock, sold at the IPO price but not registered in the offering.

The "$61 billion" figure is simple multiplication: 825M shares times $74 is $61.05B. But 23.6M of those shares belong to existing holders, and Anthropic receives nothing for them.

At the midpoint Anthropic's own gross from the public shares is $59.30B, and the filing's dilution table agrees to the dollar ($59,300,000,196). Add the $26.0B placement and the gross is $85.3B. The filing's estimate of net proceeds is $84.6B, which implies about $0.7B of discounts and expenses (my arithmetic; I did not find the discount percentage in the sections I read, so I am not judging whether that is high or low).

One more check on valuation. Press reports quote $1.26 trillion to $1.41 trillion. I could not reproduce that from the filed share count. Multiplying 13.09B shares by $70 to $78 gives $0.92 trillion to $1.02 trillion. The press figures presumably include options, restricted stock and other dilutive securities that I did not add up, so I would treat the valuation range as "reported, not reproduced."

This is the part I found most useful to read slowly. The S-1 says Anthropic has entered into purchase commitments and leases with an aggregate committed spend of approximately $900.9 billion, substantially all for compute and infrastructure. The contractual obligations table in the same filing totals only $320.0B. The gap is explained in a footnote, and when you add the footnote items back, the numbers close exactly:

Half of the headline is leases that have not commenced. The filing says they are generally expected to start between 2026 and 2028, with terms from under a year to 20 years and a weighted average of about 10 years. So the number is a long-dated lease stack, mostly data center capacity that is not yet built or delivered. That matters for how to read "$900 billion": it is a ceiling on obligations over about two decades, not a bill due this year.

The filing also notes that about $451.6B is expected to be incurred before December 31, 2030, and substantially all of the remainder between 2031 and 2045. In addition there are agreements with no fixed dollar amount, which the filing excludes from the table. I have not seen those quantified in the primary text, so I would ignore any outlet that gives a total including them without citing a page.

For scale, the $3.4B gas generator order that the FT reported on November 8 (I covered it here) would be about 0.4% of this figure, if it is even counted in it. The filing does not itemize it.

Yes, and it is a small but real signal about how fast commitments are being added.

Some articles still carry the $895B figure because they were written off the earlier amendment. Check the date on whatever you read. I did not find an explanation in the filing for the specific $5.5B, so I will not guess at one.

The filing says about $70.7B of the commitments are due in the 12 months after September 30, 2026, of which about $4.2B relates to commitments with termination rights or conditions. Against that:

Cash and cash equivalents were $95.6B and short-term investments $36.3B, with another $14.8B available under the credit facility. I did not add the credit facility to the pro forma figure. I also did not subtract any burn since September 30, so the 3.1x is an upper bound.

Next to the money due soon sits the money coming in. Revenue was $33.2B in the nine months ended September 30, 2026, about $17.0B in the third quarter alone, and run-rate revenue was about $81B on October 31, up $10B from September 30. Compute and infrastructure expense was $18.8B for the nine months, which is 56.6% of revenue, down from 172.1% in the same period of 2025.

Here is the number I would watch. Annualizing nine-month compute expense gives about $25.1B a year. The $70.7B due in the next 12 months is about 2.8 times that (my arithmetic). Not all of that is operating expense, since part is lease and capacity payments, so this is a rough indicator rather than a forecast. But it says the cost base is expected to step up sharply within a year, and the next filings will show whether revenue keeps pace. The S-1 itself says its revenue is primarily consumption-based and often not under long-term contracts, while the commitments run for years.

The nine-month net loss was $104.0B, which looks alarming until you read the lines above it. Operating loss was $1.26B. The large number comes from a $94.3B change in the fair value of convertible notes, derivative liabilities and SAFEs, which is about 91% of the net loss. The filing ties the accumulated deficit of $156.2B to the same effect, including $135.1B from fair-value changes driven by the rising enterprise valuation. In other words, the bigger the valuation, the larger the paper loss on its own convertible notes. This is an accounting item rather than cash going out, but I am relying on the filing's description and have not modeled what happens to those instruments at the IPO.

The obligations table gives the schedule for the fixed $320.0B, and it ramps hard.

Spending in 2028 to 2030 averages about $45.8B a year before the $148.4B tail. The timing reflects delivery schedules as of September 30, and the filing warns that because much of the capacity has not been delivered, both timing and, in some cases, amounts may differ.

I am not a securities analyst, so I will keep this to the engineering-adjacent readings.

How much money is Anthropic raising in its IPO? At the $74.00 midpoint, Anthropic receives about $59.3B gross from 801,351,354 new Class A shares. A separate $26.0B private placement adds to that, and the filing estimates $84.6B in net proceeds for both combined. The total offering of 825M shares is $61.05B at the midpoint, but 23.6M shares are sold by existing holders.

What is the $900.9 billion in Anthropic's S-1? It is the aggregate committed spend on purchase commitments and leases, substantially all for compute and infrastructure. It adds up from $320.0B of fixed obligations in the table, $451.2B of leases not yet commenced, $105.7B of commitments signed after September 30, and $24.0B of short-term minimums.

How much of the commitments is due in the next year? About $70.7B in the 12 months following September 30, 2026, of which about $4.2B relates to commitments with termination rights or conditions. That was $69.8B in the previous amendment.

Why is the net loss so large if the operating loss is small? For the nine months to September 30, 2026, the operating loss was $1.26B and the net loss $104.0B. About $94.3B came from changes in the fair value of convertible notes, derivative liabilities and SAFEs, a non-cash item linked to the rising valuation.

Who is buying in the private placement? Google, NVIDIA, AMD, Broadcom, Micron, Microsoft and TSMC would together buy $26.0B of Class N stock at the IPO price, per the filing. At the midpoint, Google and NVIDIA are about $10.0B each.

¿Cuánto vale un punto en un ranking de IA? Auditar los rankings oficiales de Hugging Face con Python

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hugging Face publica rankings oficiales por benchmark, y todos se pueden descargar por API sin clave. Los descargué el 11 de noviembre de 2026 (59 benchmarks, 1.389 resultados) y encontré tres cosas que cambian cómo se lee un primer puesto. Primera: ninguno de los 1.389 resultados lleva el sello verified, porque ese sello exige una evaluación ejecutada en HF Jobs con inspect-ai y hoy nadie lo tiene en estas tablas. Segunda: solo 590 resultados (42,5 %) traen una nota con el protocolo de evaluación, y 26 de esas notas mencionan voto mayoritario. Tercera: en GPQA Diamond (198 preguntas) una pregunta vale 0,505 puntos, así que el 1.º y el 2.º puesto, separados por 0,24 puntos, están separados por menos de media pregunta. Abajo está el script completo (unas 30 líneas, solo biblioteca estándar) para repetirlo hoy y comprobar que los números cambian.

Aviso de entrada: esta cuenta es de VIDRAFT y en estas tablas aparecen modelos nuestros (FINAL-Bench/Darwin). Los incluyo con el mismo rasero que a los demás: sus resultados son autorreportados, no verificados, y en tres casos concretos (AIME 2026, HMMT y GPQA) explico abajo por qué su cifra de la tabla es la más favorable de las que publicamos.

Un dataset con la etiqueta benchmark:official y un archivo eval.yaml define una o varias tareas. Los resultados no se envían a un formulario central: cada modelo guarda un YAML en su propio repositorio, dentro de .eval_results/, y Hugging Face los agrega en la tabla del benchmark. Según la documentación del Hub, un resultado lleva como mínimo el dataset, la tarea y el valor, y opcionalmente una source (por ejemplo, un enlace a trazas o a un paper), una fecha y una notes con los detalles de la evaluación.

El sello verified se concede cuando hay un verifyToken válido, es decir, cuando la evaluación corrió en HF Jobs con inspect-ai. Un resultado enviado por pull request abierta se muestra como community. Todo lo demás es lo que el autor del modelo declara en su ficha.

Esto no es una crítica a nadie: es un diseño descentralizado y la propia documentación lo llama un trabajo en curso. Pero implica que una tabla es un inventario de declaraciones, y que leerla bien exige mirar tres campos que casi nadie mira: verified, notes y el tamaño del benchmark.

La lista de benchmarks oficiales sale de un filtro de la API y cada ranking está en /api/datasets//leaderboard. Este es el script completo. Solo usa la biblioteca estándar:

La salida que obtuve el 11 de noviembre de 2026 a las 11:28 (hora de Corea):

Los tamaños n salen de fuentes públicas: 198 (GPQA Diamond), 12.032 (MMLU-Pro, división de test), 30 (AIME 2026), 33 (HMMT febrero 2026) y 500 (SWE-bench Verified) aparecen en las notas de los propios resultados o en los metadatos del dataset, y 2.500 (HLE) en la nota del resultado de Kimi-K3.1-Pro y en la documentación del Hub. Los números cambian a diario porque entran modelos nuevos; que tu ejecución no coincida al decimal es lo esperado.

Significa que, hoy, cada cifra de estas tablas es lo que cada equipo declara en su ficha. No significa que sean falsas. Muchas traen una nota de protocolo detallada y una fuente enlazada, y eso permite repetirlas. Pero la tabla no puede decirte que alguien las repitió.

La gráfica muestra otra cara del mismo asunto: 799 de los 1.389 resultados (57,5 %) no traen ninguna nota. Sin nota no sabes si hubo herramientas, cuántas muestras, qué presupuesto de razonamiento ni si la cifra es de una sola corrida. Entre los que sí la traen, 92 mencionan tools (con herramientas o sin ellas, no-tools) y 26 voto mayoritario. Compararlos sin leerlas es comparar protocolos distintos como si fueran el mismo.

Aquí está el cálculo que casi nunca se hace. Si un benchmark tiene n preguntas, un acierto más sube la nota 100 / n puntos. En GPQA Diamond son 0,505 puntos por pregunta. Un 94,44 equivale exactamente a 187 de 198, y un 93,43 a 185 de 198: dos preguntas de diferencia.

El primer puesto de GPQA en la tabla es 94,44 y el segundo 94,2. La diferencia, 0,24 puntos, son 0,5 preguntas. Además, 94,2 no corresponde a un número entero de aciertos (daría 186,5 de 198), así que no es una sola corrida contada pregunta a pregunta, y su ficha no trae nota de protocolo. No es un reproche: simplemente no se puede saber, y esa es la cuestión.

Para estimar el ruido de muestreo uso el intervalo de Wilson al 95 %, que se comporta bien con proporciones cercanas al 100 % y con n pequeño (el intervalo normal clásico se sale del rango). Para el 94,44 de GPQA da de 90,3 a 96,9. Para el 100 de AIME 2026 con 30 problemas, de 88,6 a 100: un 100 sobre 30 problemas es compatible con un modelo que falla uno de cada nueve.

La regla práctica que sale de la gráfica: cuando el margen entre el 1.º y el 2.º es menor que el ancho del intervalo, no hay base estadística para ordenarlos. Eso pasa en GPQA, AIME 2026, HMMT y SWE-bench Verified. En cambio, en MMLU-Pro (1,63 puntos con 12.032 preguntas) y en HLE (6,1 puntos con 2.500) el margen sí supera el ruido de muestreo. Con la salvedad de que esta es una aproximación conservadora: al comparar dos modelos sobre las mismas preguntas, un test pareado es más sensible que mirar intervalos por separado. Pero necesita las respuestas por pregunta, que la tabla no publica.

Este es el caso donde la nota importa más, y lo vemos en nuestros propios resultados. Las notas de Darwin-180B-RSI en AIME 2026 y HMMT febrero 2026 dicen que el valor del ranking es el voto mayoritario de 16 muestras (maj@16) y, en la misma nota, dan la exactitud media de esas 16 muestras: 98,75 en AIME y 96,59 en HMMT, frente al 100 de la tabla en ambos.

(El eje vertical de esta gráfica empieza en 90 para que se vea la diferencia; no leas las alturas como proporciones.)

Ninguna de las dos cifras es incorrecta, pero responden preguntas distintas. El voto mayoritario dice qué responde el modelo si pagas 16 muestras por pregunta y te quedas con la más frecuente. La media dice qué te toca en una sola llamada. Para un producto con latencia y coste por token, la segunda es la más cercana a tu experiencia. La tabla muestra la primera. Y en GPQA ocurre lo mismo: el 94,44 de Darwin-180B-RSI es un voto mayoritario de hasta 16 muestras, mientras que Darwin-397B-ZTC figura con 93,43 en el puesto 4 con decodificación voraz y una sola muestra. Son dos protocolos en la misma columna.

Tomo de ahí una conclusión que vale para nuestra propia comunicación: un primer puesto por 0,24 puntos en 198 preguntas, con voto mayoritario, no demuestra que el modelo sea mejor que el segundo. Lo que sí es verificable por cualquiera es el dato del repositorio: valor, protocolo declarado y fuente enlazada.

Una lista corta de hábitos que me funcionan al leer cualquier ranking:

Nuestros resultados en estas tablas están en la misma situación que los demás: 27 filas en total, 11 primeros puestos más un empate a 100 en AIME 2026 (donde figuramos segundos por orden de lista), todos con verified en false. Si quieres contrastar cualquiera, el script de arriba te da la fila, la nota y la fuente en segundos.

¿Dónde se pide la lista de rankings oficiales de Hugging Face? Con GET https://huggingface.co/api/datasets?filter=benchmark:official&limit=200. Cada ranking está en GET /api/datasets//leaderboard, sin clave de API. El día de esta medición eran 59 benchmarks, 53 con resultados y 6 vacíos.

¿Qué significa verified en un resultado de Hugging Face? Que hay un verifyToken válido: la evaluación se ejecutó en HF Jobs con inspect-ai. En los 1.389 resultados que descargué el 11 de noviembre de 2026, ninguno lo tiene.

¿Por qué usar el intervalo de Wilson y no el normal? Porque con proporciones cerca del 100 % y pocas preguntas (30 en AIME), el intervalo normal puede pasarse de 100. Wilson se mantiene dentro del rango y cubre mejor con n pequeño.

¿Un margen menor que el intervalo prueba que dos modelos son iguales? No: solo dice que esta tabla no alcanza para ordenarlos. Un test pareado sobre las respuestas por pregunta puede separar modelos que los intervalos individuales no separan.

¿Cuál es la diferencia entre voto mayoritario y exactitud media? El voto mayoritario (maj@k) toma la respuesta más frecuente entre k muestras; la exactitud media promedia el acierto de cada muestra. La primera suele ser mayor y cuesta k veces más cómputo por pregunta.

¿Estas tablas sirven para elegir un modelo para producción? Sirven para preseleccionar. La decisión final debería apoyarse en una evaluación propia con tus datos, tu latencia y un protocolo de una sola llamada si eso es lo que usarás.

Fuentes: documentación de resultados de evaluación del Hub de Hugging Face, ranking de GPQA, ranking de AIME 2026.

TensorRT-LLM 1.4 Makes trtllm-serve Run 8 HTTP Frontends by Default: RAM Cost, Connection Balance, and What Still Differs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

TensorRT-LLM 1.4.0rc3 (GitHub release 2026-11-06) flips one default that changes the shape of your deployment: trtllm-serve now starts 8 HTTP frontend processes instead of 1. The release notes mark it BREAKING (PR #19379). I read that PR, the earlier prototype PR #16523, the metrics fix #19394, the current docs on main, and the release notes, then ran two small simulations of my own. I did not run a GPU workload.

The CLI default for --num_serve_frontends on the trtllm-serve serve subcommand went from 1 to 8. The launcher process owns the engine. The other 7 frontends re-exec the command line, attach to the same executor, and bind the same port with SO_REUSEPORT, so clients still see one URL.

Three details from the PR are easy to miss:

The same release candidate carries two other BREAKING entries (#21036 removes unused LlmRequest fields, #21041 removes obsolete TensorRT-backend compatibility surfaces). Those are API removals. The frontend default is the one that changes what a plain launch does on your host.

Every request on a single trtllm-serve process goes through one asyncio event loop: json.loads, pydantic validation, and then every streamed chunk written back. The prototype PR (#16523, merged 2026-07-22) reported this on a DeepSeek V4 disaggregated generation worker:

Doubling concurrency from 512 to 1,024 roughly doubled the delay. Doubling it again multiplied it by about 14, while decode step time stayed flat. The PR author called this an M/M/1-style blow-up, and I agree with the shape. In a single-server queue the mean wait grows like utilization divided by one minus utilization, so it looks harmless until the loop approaches saturation and then explodes. Adding GPU capacity does nothing for it, because the GPU is not the queue.

That is why eight processes help: the same parsing and streaming work is spread over eight event loops and eight GILs. The PR says the setting was already the production configuration in the authors' agentic disaggregated setups, with 8 on context workers and 4 on generation workers.

The kernel assigns each new connection to one of the listening sockets, effectively by hashing the connection tuple. The docs say it plainly: each request is handled entirely by the frontend that accepted its connection. Once a client holds a keep-alive connection, all of its requests land on the same frontend.

So the question that matters is how many long-lived connections your clients open. I modeled it with the standard library only. The assumptions are mine and deliberately simple: connections are assigned uniformly at random to 8 frontends, every connection carries equal load, and the total offered load equals 3 single-process event loops (so one frontend alone would be hopelessly saturated). Perfect balance would put each frontend at 0.375 utilization, a mean M/M/1 wait of 0.6 service times.

I ran 20,000 draws per connection count with a fixed seed:

With 1,024 connections you are within 13% of perfect balance. With 16 you are at a median utilization of 0.75, a mean wait of 3.0 service times against the balanced 0.6, and about one draw in thirteen saturates a frontend. This is an illustration, not a measurement of TensorRT-LLM. Real traffic is lumpier than equal-weight connections (a long streaming response weighs more than a short one), which I expect makes the tail worse, but I have not measured that. If your traffic comes through a router or gateway with a small connection pool, count its connections to this server before assuming 8 frontends means 8x headroom.

The PR's verification section is the only measurement I found, so I use it as is. One container with 1x GB300, Qwen3-0.6B, launched the way that cluster runs production:

Resident memory was about 1.99 GB per attached frontend and about 2.5 GB for the launcher. Every extra frontend is a full Python process that imports TensorRT-LLM and loads the tokenizer, which is what the docs warn about. The arithmetic for the default is 2.5 + 7 x 1.99 = 16.43 GB, about 13.9 GB more than a single frontend.

Two things to keep in mind. First, this is one run on a tiny model, so treat 1.99 GB as an order of magnitude, since a larger tokenizer or extra imports will move it. Second, a container memory limit sized around a single serving process will now get OOM-killed during startup. Attached frontends hold no GPU memory: in the PR, nvidia-smi listed only the worker (58.7 GiB including KV cache) and the launcher (748 MiB), identical with 1 or 8 frontends.

This is the part I almost got wrong. The body of #19379 lists a caveat: /metrics, /prometheus/metrics and /health are answered by whichever frontend accepted the connection, so scrapes see one frontend's share and counters appear to reset. It cites a measurement of trtllm_request_success_total showing 5 distinct values (6, 7, 8 and 10 among them) for 64 requests across 32 scrapes.

But #19394, "make multi-frontend metrics, profiling and liveness launcher-owned", was merged on 2026-10-28, two days before the default flip, and I confirmed with the compare API that both land first in rc3 (neither is in rc2). It reports the same 32 scrapes returning one identical value (64) afterwards. The mechanism: the launcher sets one shared PROMETHEUS_MULTIPROC_DIR before it spawns children, iteration-derived series come from the launcher only, and attached frontends forward GET /metrics to the launcher over a Unix socket. The current docs on main match that: /metrics is forwarded and /prometheus/metrics covers every frontend.

Why does this matter to you? Because Prometheus treats any decrease in a counter as a reset. To show what the sharded behavior would have done to a dashboard, I simulated 64 requests landing on 8 per-process counters, scraped at random every second request:

With shared counters the increase is the true value, 62 between the first and last scrape, every time. With per-frontend counters the 5th to 95th percentile spread is 47 to 86 (minimum 23, maximum 111), and 38% of runs were off by more than 10. Illustrative again, but it is why a build without #19394 and with 8 frontends is a bad combination for anything alerting on request rates.

You can check your own server in a few seconds. This probe scrapes a counter repeatedly and flags any decrease. I tested it against a mock HTTP server that I wrote in both modes (it reported consistent for shared counters and a drop count for sharded ones). I have not pointed it at a real TensorRT-LLM server.

Send some traffic while it runs. If the counter is flat you cannot tell the difference.

Per the current docs and the PRs, these remain:

The /health row is the one I would test in your environment. A load balancer health check that lands on a different frontend each time is exactly the kind of thing that passes in staging and flaps in production.

I ran no GPU workload and no TensorRT-LLM server. The latency, memory and startup numbers are quoted from the PRs, and I attribute them as such. The two simulations are my own, use simplifying assumptions stated above, and should be read as intuition about the mechanism, not as predictions for your fleet. My statements about which rc contains which PR come from the GitHub compare API and the rc3 release notes on 2026-11-11.

Does running 8 frontends use more GPU memory? No. In the PR's test, attached frontends held no GPU memory. nvidia-smi listed only the worker and the launcher, in both the 8-frontend and 1-frontend runs.

How do I go back to a single frontend? Pass --num_serve_frontends 1 or set num_serve_frontends: 1 in the --config YAML. The YAML value overrides the new CLI default.

Will pip install tensorrt-llm give me this default? Not today. The latest non-prerelease on PyPI is 1.2.1. The change is in the 1.4.0rc3 release candidate (also uploaded to PyPI on 2026-11-06) and on main.

How many frontends should I run? The PR does not give a formula. Its production configs use 8 on context workers and 4 on generation workers. I would start from the free CPU cores and the RAM budget above, then measure request-handling latency at your real concurrency.

Is the stateful Responses API store gone? It is disabled whenever more than one frontend runs, because it lives in each process's memory. With --num_serve_frontends 1 it works as before.

Can another process steal traffic through SO_REUSEPORT? The docs say the launcher refuses a port that another server already uses, which is the guard against a second server of the same user joining the port.

AI training helped some women more job interviews, but not as many as their rivals got

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In a hiring test, AI training lifted interview calls for underrepresented women from 11 to 14 percent, while others got 25 percent without it.

Learning a popular skill like AI is supposed to help you get a job. But a new study found that for women who already get fewer interviews, having an AI certificate only helped a little bit.

In a London hiring experiment, it lifted their rate of interview invitations from about 11 to 14 percent.

Prospective employers invited comparable women outside those groups, with no certificate at all, to interview 25 percent of the time. The certificate narrowed the gap, but most of the gap was still there.

Nick Drydakis, an economist at Anglia Ruskin University (ARU) in Cambridge, England, ran the experiment in 2025. He directs the university’s Centre for Inclusive Societies and Economies.

Governments, employers and training providers have been promoting AI training as a route into work. Most research on AI and hiring, though, has looked at bias inside screening software.

Drydakis asked what employers do when the applicant is the one with AI skills.

Between January and June, his team answered 1,016 real job ads in London, sending three matched applications to each one. That came to more than 3,000 applications in all.

Every applicant was a fictional woman applying for a full-time private-sector job that didn’t require a degree. Each held a Level 3 vocational qualification, a practical course usually started between ages 16 and 18, plus a one-year apprenticeship.

The study ran as four separate experiments. In each, one application came from a woman in the majority group, and two came from a woman in an underrepresented group.

The underrepresented groups were Black British women, women aged 50 instead of 28, lesbian women, and women who disclosed being on the autism spectrum.

Sexual orientation showed up as a partner’s name, Helen rather than George. Autism showed up as a short line such as, “I am on the autism spectrum and known for my strong focus and attention to detail.”

One of the two applications from the underrepresented group also listed a six-month AI training certificate.

Its description covered using AI tools to automate office paperwork and handle data, aimed at the routine digital work these jobs involve.

Before any applications went out, 71 people, including HR staff, employers, students and hiring researchers, checked that each signal read clearly and that the matched applications looked equally strong otherwise.

Every application went out within a day of the ad appearing, and the team saw no sign that any employer noticed the matched sets.

Across all four experiments, prospective employers invited women from the majority groups to interview 25 percent of the time. Women from the underrepresented groups, with matching qualifications and no certificate, were invited 11 percent of the time.

The gap appeared in every experiment. Because the applications were matched, Drydakis concluded that employers were responding differently at the screening stage.

With the AI certificate, the underrepresented women’s rate rose to 14 percent. That’s about 28 percent more invitations than the same women got without it, yet still far below the majority group’s 25 percent.

“AI training can help open doors, but it should not be viewed as a substitute for fair recruitment practices,” Drydakis said.

The boost wasn’t the same everywhere. Women who disclosed autism were invited about 7 percent of the time without the certificate and 11 percent with it, against 24 percent for women who didn’t disclose.

Lesbian women’s invitation rate rose from about 13 to 18 percent, against 26 percent for heterosexual women. Both of those gains held up in the statistical tests.

For women aged 50, the rise from about 10 to 12 percent was smaller, and its statistical support depended on the test used.

The invitation rate for Black British women showed a small change, from 15 to 17 percent. Because of this small change, the experiment cannot determine whether the certificate provided any benefit at all.

The interview invitations also differed in what the jobs paid. Underrepresented women without the certificate were more often invited by employers advertising lower wages, about 7 percent lower per hour on average.

With the certificate, that pay gap shrank to about 4 percent. It didn’t disappear.

So the certificate helped in two ways, more calls and better-paid ones. However, neither brought the women level with the majority group.

Drydakis has argued in earlier work that AI skills act as a form of AI capital, something workers can build that pays off in the job market. This experiment fits that idea, up to a point.

“If we want technological change to create opportunities for everyone, investment in digital skills must be matched by strong efforts to tackle discrimination and ensure qualifications are assessed consistently across all applicant groups,” he said.

No majority-group applicant ever carried the AI certificate. That means the study can’t say whether the training would help every applicant just as much. If it did, the gap between groups wouldn’t shrink at all.

The study also stopped at the interview invitation. It didn’t follow job offers, actual pay, or how anyone was treated at work. Nor can it tell whether employers acted on prejudice or on guesses about who would do the job well.

All the applicants were women seeking jobs without a degree requirement in one city, so the results may not hold for graduate jobs, men, or other places.

A certificate that stood out in 2025 may also become routine as AI training spreads, and repeating the experiment in a few years would show whether its value holds up.

The full study was published in the journal Industrial Relations: A Journal of Economy and Society.

Top Five AI Crypto Projects to Watch as AI Adoption Grows

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Yash is a crypto analyst specializing in price analysis, predictions, and in-depth research reports. He combines technical indicators with on-chain data to uncover market trends and potential breakouts. His sharp insights help readers navigate the crypto market with confidence. Whether it’s Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

Yash is a crypto analyst specializing in price analysis, predictions, and in-depth research reports. He combines technical indicators with on-chain data to uncover market trends and potential breakouts. His sharp insights help readers navigate the crypto market with confidence. Whether it’s Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

Yash is a crypto analyst specializing in price analysis, predictions, and in-depth research reports. He combines technical indicators with on-chain data to uncover market trends and potential breakouts. His sharp insights help readers navigate the crypto market with confidence. Whether it’s Bitcoin or emerging altcoins, Yash breaks it down with clarity and precision.

Artificial intelligence is evolving beyond conversational tools into autonomous systems capable of conducting research, writing code, analyzing information, and completing complex workflows.

The release of Artificial Analysis’s dashboard’s metric known as ‘Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v5’ highlights this progress through more demanding benchmarks, private evaluation datasets, and improved testing of real-world AI capabilities.

The findings reveal three important trends: leading AI models are becoming more capable, computational requirements are increasing, and significant performance differences remain between proprietary and open-weight models.

These developments are making a real difference in society and could create opportunities for AI crypto projects focused on decentralized computing, machine intelligence, and even autonomous AI agents.

Mong crypto sector, there are five best projects that stand out for their involvement in these areas: Bittensor (TAO), Render Network (RENDER), Akash Network (AKT), Artificial Superintelligence Alliance (FET), and Virtuals Protocol (VIRTUAL).

Their long-term potential will depend on real-world adoption, competitive infrastructure, and whether growing AI activity translates into sustainable demand for their tokens.

Released on October 27, 2026, Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v5 introduced two new benchmarks and upgrades three existing evaluations.

Approximately 60% of the index now relies on private test sets or held-out answers, reducing the influence of familiarity with publicly available benchmark questions.

The index evaluates four categories, each carrying 25% of the overall weighting:

The overall rankings place Claude Opus 5.5 at 58 points, GPT-6 Astra at 57, and GPT-6.1 Sol at 55. Gemini 4 Argon follows at 50.

Performance varies across individual evaluations. Claude Opus 5.5 leads FrontierCode 2.0 at 46% and HLE-Diamond at 68%, while GPT-6 Astra leads Terminal-Bench-Science at 63% and the CritPt v2 hard subset at 72%. This demonstrates that no single model dominates every category.

Announcing Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v5, featuring 2 new benchmarks and 3 upgrades. For the first time, the majority of the Index is built on private datasetsThe Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index is our composite measure of model capabilities across tasks… pic.twitter.com/oA4tsXQOC6

The strongest model without released weights, Kimi K3.1 Pro, scores 46 on the index. Among models with released weights, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 and GLM-5.3 lead at 32.

The gap between the overall leader and the strongest open-weight model has increased from three points in version 4.3 to 12 points in version 5.

This presents a challenge for decentralized AI infrastructure, where access to competitive models is essential for delivering useful services.

One of the most important findings concerns the resources required to complete increasingly complex AI tasks.

Artificial Analysis reports that median output tokens per task increased from approximately 35,000 in version 4.3 to 59,000 in version 5, representing an increase of roughly 69%.

Agentic evaluations account for approximately 88% of median output-token usage despite representing 55% of the index’s weighting.

The cost per task also varies significantly, ranging from $0.96 for GPT-6.1 Terra to $13.04 for Claude Fable 5.1 among the ten highest-scoring models shown.

These findings highlight the growing importance of affordable and reliable computing infrastructure.

The Intelligence Index demonstrates that AI development is increasingly focused on completing practical tasks rather than simply generating responses.

Three developments are particularly relevant.

Coding and software development: FrontierCode 2.0 evaluates 132 tasks across real open-source repositories, measuring whether an AI agent produces changes that meet the requirements for a maintainer to merge them. Claude Opus 5.5 leads with 46%.

Scientific research: Terminal-Bench-Science evaluates 70 research tasks performed in a terminal environment. GPT-6 Astra leads at 63%, followed by Claude Opus 5.5 at 59%.

Knowledge and reasoning: HLE-Diamond evaluates 1,000 questions covering reasoning and knowledge. Claude Opus 5.5 leads at 68%, although knowledge questions remain more challenging than reasoning questions.

Together, these results through analyzing metrics clearly highlight the growing capabilities of agentic AI while demonstrating that accuracy, reliability, and computational efficiency remain important challenges.

As autonomous agents become more capable, demand could increase for computing resources, specialized AI services, data access, and automated transactions.

The development of advanced AI systems creates opportunities across several areas of the cryptocurrency ecosystem.

However, AI progress does not automatically translate into higher token prices. The opportunity depends on whether blockchain-based projects can attract genuine customers and compete with centralized alternatives.

Advanced AI models require substantial computing resources, particularly for inference, research, and autonomous agent execution.

Decentralized GPU marketplaces could provide alternative access to computing capacity while allowing hardware providers to monetize unused resources.

Render (RENDER) relies on a Burn-and-Mint Equilibrium (BME) model to balance computing demand with RENDER token burns, while Akash (AKT) leverages Cosmos SDK-based reverse auctions to lower provisioning costs. The success of these compute protocols hinges on real paid workload volume, network uptime, and GPU hardware availability rather than token speculation.

Autonomous AI agents require friction-free, trustless payment rails to purchase compute capacity, scrape dataset APIs, and execute cross-chain transactions without human intervention. Standard banking APIs and fiat payment rails cannot support self-custodial software execution.

Blockchain networks could support programmable payments and coordination between independent agents.

FET and Virtuals are relevant to this developing market. These provide the programmable micro-transaction layer via smart contracts and native tokens, allowing autonomous software entities to hold private keys, manage capital, and execute automated machine-to-machine payments.

However, conventional payment systems and centralized APIs will remain competitive alternatives.

Different AI models perform better at different tasks, creating opportunities for specialized intelligence services.

Bittensor attempts to coordinate independent contributors through subnet-based markets, rewarding participants for providing useful digital services.

Its long-term success depends on whether its incentive mechanisms reward genuine utility and attract sustainable external demand.

The most important distinction in evaluating AI crypto projects is the difference between product adoption and token demand.

A network may attract users without generating meaningful economic value for its token.

These factors provide a stronger basis for evaluating long-term potential than AI-related branding alone.

The following projects represent five different areas of the AI and blockchain ecosystem. They are selected for their technological relevance, not ranked by expected investment returns.

Category: Decentralized machine intelligence

Bittensor creates decentralized markets for digital commodities, including machine intelligence, computing, and other specialized services.

Its subnet architecture allows independent contributors to provide outputs, while validators evaluate performance and distribute incentives.

TAO is central to Bittensor’s incentive and network-security model. Its economic design connects token distribution with subnet participation, while validators evaluate the quality of contributions within individual subnets. Subnet-level incentives add another layer to this structure, making it important to examine how emissions are distributed and whether rewards reflect measurable utility. The key metric is whether subnet activity attracts external demand rather than depending primarily on token incentives.

Moreover, On-chain subnet emission data further illustrates the network’s incentive distribution, with SN51 currently receiving 17.07% of emissions, followed by SN64 at 9.34% and SN4 at 8.04%, highlighting the varying allocation of TAO incentives across leading subnets.

Why it matters: Bittensor offers an alternative approach to developing and coordinating AI services without relying entirely on centralized providers.

Bittensor uses its native consensus algorithm (Yuma Consensus) to rank and reward subnet contributors. Subnets act as competitive machine learning markets where miners compete by providing optimized model outputs, and validators score their performance.

TAO tokens are minted per block and allocated dynamically across subnets based on performance metrics, incentivizing open-source AI development outside corporate walled gardens.

Key risk: Subnet quality, incentive design, token emissions, and the ability to attract genuine customer demand.

Category: Decentralized GPU infrastructure

Render Network connects GPU providers with customers requiring computing resources. Its ecosystem extends beyond graphics rendering into selected AI-related applications, including machine learning, inference, and generative AI.

RENDER uses a burn-and-mint equilibrium model designed to connect network usage with token economics. In broad terms, tokens are burned in connection with rendering work, while network participants receive emissions-based rewards. This creates a potential link between paid workload demand and token supply dynamics.

The important factors to monitor are actual network usage, burn activity, reward emissions and whether demand for AI-related workloads becomes a meaningful part of network activity.

Moreover, Render’s cumulative burn has reached approximately 2.26 million RENDER as of November 6, 2026, providing an on-chain indicator of token consumption associated with network activity.

Why it matters: Growing AI workloads could increase demand for accessible GPU infrastructure and distributed computing services.

Key risk: Competition from centralized cloud providers and the need to demonstrate meaningful AI workload adoption.

Akash Network operates a marketplace for computing resources and has expanded into AI inference and GPU workloads through developments such as AkashML.

AKT plays a role in Akash’s decentralized marketplace economy, including network participation, staking and economic coordination.

Customers use the marketplace to obtain computing resources, while providers compete to supply infrastructure. The important economic question is how deployment payments, settlement processes, staking requirements and network fees translate into sustained AKT demand. Marketplace growth is more meaningful when it creates recurring economic activity rather than relying mainly on incentives.

Moreover, Akash Network’s reported GPU capacity has increased to 419, indicating growing available computing capacity within its decentralized GPU marketplace.

Why it matters: Developers may increasingly seek flexible and cost-effective infrastructure to deploy AI applications and autonomous agents.

Key risk: Infrastructure reliability, developer adoption, cloud-provider competition, and the relationship between network usage and AKT demand.

The Artificial Superintelligence Alliance brings together the Fetch.ai, Ocean Protocol, and SingularityNET ecosystems under an AI-focused initiative.

Its technology focuses on decentralized AI services, autonomous agents, and digital transactions.

FET is the ecosystem’s principal token for coordinating participation across its AI-focused infrastructure and services. Its long-term relevance depends on the extent to which agent-related services, network transactions, staking and other ecosystem activities require or create demand for the token. It is important to distinguish technical adoption of autonomous agents from actual FET usage. The token’s economic case becomes stronger when recurring services and transactions create measurable utility.

Moreover, The network’s transaction data records 36,429,122 total transactions, including 77,534 transactions over the past 30 days, offering a measurable view of recent on-chain activity.

Why it matters: Increasingly capable AI agents could create demand for systems that coordinate services, exchange information, and execute automated transactions.

Key risk: Agent adoption does not automatically translate into FET demand. Token utility and sustained commercial usage remain essential.

Virtuals Protocol focuses on creating and coordinating AI agents within tokenized digital ecosystems.

Its approach connects AI applications with economic mechanisms that allow agents and their users to participate in digital markets.

VIRTUAL is connected to the protocol’s AI-agent economy, where tokenized mechanisms support agent creation, participation and economic activity. Its token model should be assessed through the relationship between agent launches, transactions, liquidity mechanisms and recurring user demand. A growing number of agents does not necessarily mean sustainable token value capture. The key question is whether agents generate ongoing economic activity that requires VIRTUAL, rather than relying primarily on speculative trading or launch-related demand.

Moreover, The actively traded Virtuals agent market currently represents an estimated market capitalization of $337.27 million, based on the fully diluted valuation methodology for agents meeting the specified trading-volume threshold.

Why it matters: The growth of autonomous AI agents could create opportunities for platforms supporting specialized digital services and agent-based economies.

Key risk: Speculative activity, competition, limited recurring demand, and uncertainty around long-term token value capture.

Each project addresses a different requirement within the emerging AI economy. Their prospects will depend on product adoption, competitive positioning, token economics, and their ability to generate sustainable demand.

The Intelligence Index v5 findings point toward an AI industry increasingly focused on autonomous systems, complex workflows, and computationally intensive applications.

This could create opportunities for decentralized infrastructure and AI-focused blockchain networks.

Three developments are particularly important.

1. Growing demand for computing: More sophisticated AI applications could increase demand for GPUs and inference infrastructure, potentially benefiting Render and Akash.

2. Expansion of autonomous agents: AI agents may increasingly interact with digital services and execute transactions, creating opportunities for FET and Virtuals.

3. Specialized intelligence markets: Demand for task-specific AI services could support decentralized networks such as Bittensor.

However, centralized AI providers remain strong competitors. They offer established infrastructure, integrated services, and operational reliability.

There is also a downside scenario: AI adoption could accelerate without decentralized networks capturing meaningful economic activity.

Consequently, the future of AI crypto will depend less on technological narratives and more on real-world usage, competitive services, and sustainable token economics.

Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v5 highlights the rapid development of AI capabilities, particularly in coding, scientific research, and autonomous workflows.

At the same time, the findings reveal increasing computational requirements and a significant performance gap between leading proprietary models and released open-weight alternatives.

These developments create potential opportunities for AI crypto projects involved in decentralized computing, specialized intelligence, and autonomous agents.

Bittensor, Render, Akash, Artificial Superintelligence Alliance, and Virtuals represent five different approaches to this emerging market.

Their long-term prospects will depend on whether they can convert technological relevance into real adoption and sustainable token demand.

The broader outlook for AI crypto is promising, but technological progress alone is not enough. The projects most likely to establish lasting positions will be those that solve genuine problems, attract paying users, and create measurable economic value through their networks.

Tajianna Roberts leads No. 7 Louisville past Lindenwood 71-35

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

LOUISVILLE, Ky. (AP) — Tajianna Roberts scored 15 points as No. 7 Louisville had little trouble dispatching Lindenwood 71-35 on Tuesday night.

It was a hot-and-cold night for the Cardinals offensively. They made their first six shots in the second quarter to take a 37-13 lead after Grace Mbugua's layup with 5:26 left in the first half.

Then after missing five straight, Louisville (4-0) ended the half on a 9-0 run, making their final four shots to take a 47-18 lead into the locker room.

The Cardinals shot 40.9% (27 for 66). While they overcame a 40.8% shooting performance Sunday at Southern Indiana by shooting 11 for 32 from the 3-point arc, Louisville also struggled from the perimeter on Tuesday going 6 for 29 from long range, including 1 for 12 in the second half.

Still, they locked down the Lions (1-3) defensively. The Ohio Valley Conference school located just outside of St. Louis went 2 for 21 over a 13-plus minute stretch from 1:22 left in the first half to 8:10 remaining in the fourth quarter.

Carys Baker scored 12 points for Louisville. A three-year player for Virginia Tech, Baker surpassed 1,000 career points with a first-quarter 3-pointer that came during an 8-0 run.

Amiya Joyner shot 5 for 7 to finish with 11 points and grabbed 10 rebounds, the first double-double of the season for the former LSU forward. Freshman Ariyana Cradle also scored 11 points, and Mbugua came off the bench to add 10 points and nine rebounds.

Louisville led 62-24 with 2:41 remaining.

Violet McNece led the Lions with six points. Lindenwood shot a season-low 26% (13 for 50) and posted its worst scoring total of the season. They also turned the ball over a season-high 21 times, leading to 25 Louisville points.

Lindenwood returns home to play NAIA school Stephens College on Friday.

Louisville travels to Ball State on Thursday night.

Prediction: This Will Be AMD’s Stock Price After 1 Year

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Wall Street expects a nice jump in AMD stock over the coming year, but it could exceed analysts' expectations.

Shares of Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +1.30%) have been under pressure lately, and the company's solid third-quarter results haven't helped arrest the slide.

Specifically, AMD stock has lost 18% of its value over the past month. Concerns about a potential slowdown in the pace of the artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out have weighed heavily on tech stocks lately. This explains why the Nasdaq Composite plunged by a whopping 5.5% on Nov. 5. AMD lost over 11% of its value that day.

However, savvy investors shouldn't miss the bigger picture. AMD's latest results clearly indicate that the pace of the AI build-out isn't slowing down. In fact, I won't be surprised to see AMD emerge from its rut and surge impressively over the coming year. Let's see how much upside investors can expect from this semiconductor stock in the next 12 months.

AMD has a 12-month median price target of $650, according to 56 analysts covering the stock. That implies potential upside of 28%. What's more, 49 analysts rate AMD as a buy, which isn't surprising considering its attractive valuation and accelerating growth.

There is a strong likelihood that AMD will hit the Wall Street price target over the coming year. That's because the company is poised for stronger growth, driven by the incredible demand for its data center chips.

The company's Q3 revenue increased by 45% year over year to $13.4 billion, with the data center business playing a central role in this big surge. AMD reported a 97% increase in data center revenue from the prior-year period to a record $8.5 billion. The company is witnessing healthy growth in demand for its server central processing units (CPUs), graphics processing units (GPUs), and Helios rack-scale systems.

It is worth noting that major hyperscalers and AI companies, including Meta Platforms, OpenAI, Anthropic, Microsoft, and Oracle, are deploying AMD's data center chip systems. AMD notes that the early demand for its latest generation of Epyc server processors, codenamed Venice, is the strongest that it has seen by far. AMD's partners are poised to launch 20% more Venice server platforms compared to the previous generation Turin processors.

Similarly, AMD's MI455X data center GPUs are also gaining strong traction among customers. All this explains why AMD is on track to deliver even stronger growth in the current quarter, followed by an impressive performance in 2027.

AMD is forecasting a 66% year-over-year increase in Q4 revenue to $17 billion, a big improvement over its Q3 growth. The company's revenue in the first nine months of 2026 increased by 44% to $35.2 billion. The Q4 forecast suggests that AMD will end 2026 with a top line of at least $52.7 billion, which would be a 52% jump over last year.

For comparison, AMD's 2025 revenue jumped by 34%. The good news for AMD investors is that the company is anticipating an 80% increase in revenue in 2028. This acceleration in AMD's revenue growth next year should translate into a bigger increase in the company's bottom line, which is precisely what analysts are anticipating.

AMD EPS Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

AMD's 2026 consensus earnings per share (EPS) estimate of $8.08, as seen in the above chart, points to a 94% jump over last year's levels. So, next year's forecast suggests that AMD's bottom line is on track to more than double.

For comparison, the average earnings growth of the S&P 500 index is forecasted at 17% next year. AMD trades at 30 times forward earnings, which isn't a big premium considering that the S&P 500 index has a price-to-earnings ratio of 23. Ideally, it should trade at a significant premium to the S&P 500 index.

Assuming AMD trades at 40 times earnings after a year and its EPS reaches $17.64 (based on consensus estimates), the stock could jump to $706. That implies a 39% jump, suggesting that it can outperform Wall Street's expectations. So, investors can consider capitalizing on the recent drop in AMD's shares and buying it right away, as there is a strong chance the stock will regain momentum and jump impressively in 2027.
